A KL-divergence based test for elliptical distribution


Abstract
We conduct a KL-divergence based procedure for testing elliptical distributions. The procedure simultaneously takes into account the two defining properties of an elliptically distributed random vector: independence between length and direction, and uniform distribution of the direction. The test statistic is constructed based on the k k nearest neighbors ( k k NN) method, and two cases are considered where the mean vector and covariance matrix are known and unknown. First-order asymptotic properties of the test statistic are rigorously established by creatively utilizing sample splitting, truncation and transformation between Euclidean space and unit sphere, while avoiding assuming Fréchet differentiability of any functionals. Debiasing and variance inflation are further proposed to treat the degeneration of the influence function. Numerical implementations suggest better size and power performance than the state of the art procedures. Keywords : Elliptical distribution, Entropy, KL-divergence, Influence function, k k NN method.

Introduction

Elliptical distribution is arguably the most frequently used
semiparametric family of distributions. It includes many familiar
distributions such as normal, student t, Laplace, Cauchy, etc.
See, for example,
Fang et al. 2013
.
Often, when we have established certain statistical properties for the
normal distribution and plan to widen were the study to a more general
setting, the first thing we would inspect is the elliptical distribution
family.
Indeed, the elliptical distribution family often retains
some nice properties that originally were only discovered to hold
under normality assumption. Thus, in practice, it is crucial to
examine whether or not the elliptical distribution assumption holds.

Many works have been devoted to testing the elliptical distribution.
A significant portion of the literatures, especially in the earlier
years, are designed for testing either the spherical distribution, or the
elliptical distribution with known
mean vector and covariance matrix. These two problems are indeed
the same under rescaling. For example,

Kariya & Eaton 1977
provides several tests on
mean and/or variance in the spherical
distribution family, and show that their tests are uniformly most
powerful (UMP) and/or uniformly most
powerful and unbiased (UMPU).

King 1980
further studies these tests and shows that
the UMP/UMPU properties are retained under linear transformations,
and applies them to linear models.

Romano 1989
introduces bootstrap and randomization
tests and applies them to test a class of nonparametric hypotheses,
including spherical distributions.

Baringhaus 1991
proposes a test based on a
second-order V-statistic involving the ranks of the lengths and
transformed pairwise angles, which is
equivalent to the
L^{2}
distance between the empirical distribution and
its nearest centered spherical distribution.

Fang et al. 1993
considers projecting the data to different
directions and forming the pairwise differences between directions,
then using a
Wilcoxon-type test statistic to test for spherical symmetry. They
require the projection directions to be sufficient rich and scattered on
the unit sphere, and otherwise the procedure is not an omnibus test.

Koltchinskii & Li 1998
uses a multivariate quantile approach
to measure the spherical asymmetry, and constructs a test for
spherical distributions with unknown centers. In this sense, it no
longer requires to know the mean vector, but still requires to know
the covariance matrix when used as a test for elliptical distributions.
In practice, they propose a
bootstrap approach or Monte Carlo approach to approximate
the null distribution.

Diks & Tong 1999
proposes a Monte Carlo based test for various types
of multivariate symmetries, which can be used to test the spherical
distribution as well.

Liang et al. 2008
devises a test for spherical distribution
via the Rosenblatt transformation on each element, which only tests
a necessary condition for spherical distributions.

Einmahl & Gantner 2012
proposes an
omnibus test for spherical distribution
using the localized empirical likelihood, but it only applies to
the bivariate case.

Liang 2012
gives a test based on plots
related to the
third derivative of the empirical moment generating
function. This test also requires projecting the data to a
specific direction, and the confidence interval is computed via
Monte Carlo.

Henze et al. 2014
forms a test by checking whether the
characteristic function is constant on the unit sphere, which is
only a necessary condition for spherical distributions.

Guha & Chakraborty 2021
constructs a test statistic derived from
scale-scale plots, and uses Monte Carlo to get the reference
distribution. However, the procedure only tests whether
different directions are uniformly distributed on the unit sphere, which
is again only a necessary condition hence the test is not omnibus.

Huang & Sen 2023
further introduces a test following the
same line via optimal transport but is also not omnibus.

Banerjee & Ghosh 2024
proposes a test based on an
energy distance between the raw data and the augmented data
formed by the raw length and newly-generated direction
vectors, and the critical value is determined via
resampling.

All the testing procedures discussed above assume a known covariance
matrix, and most of them also assume a known mean vector.
When the mean and variance are not known, it is natural to
estimate them and then insert into the testing procedures.
Such a plugin procedure gives rise to significant complications
and have been studied in the literature as well.
For example,

Fang et al. 1998
proposes a test by projecting the
normalized data to different directions and
computing the skewness and kurtosis for each of them, and further
checking whether their behavior is significantly heterogeneous
while taking into account the estimation errors of the mean and variance.
However, similar to
Fang et al. 1993
, the test is not omnibus
without considering all possible directions.

Manzotti et al. 2002
uses the spherical harmonics over the
projections of the scaled residual to perform a test for the
uniform distribution of the direction vector,

Schott 2002
tests whether the fourth moments have the
structure indicated by elliptical distributions.

Babić, Gelbgras, Hallin & Ley 2021
proposes a test statistic for elliptical
distribution which is asymptoticly
\chi^{2}
for elliptical distribution.
But the test is only optimal against some generalized
skew-elliptical distributions where their test statistic is
non-central
\chi^{2}
distributed.
The three tests above take into
account the estimation of the mean and variance in deriving the
theoretical properties of the tests, but only detect certain
departures from the elliptical distribution.

Iwashita & Klar 2020
introduces a test for elliptical
distribution by only testing the uniform distribution on the Stiefel
manifold based on modified degenerate V-statistics.

Soloveychik 2023
constructs a goodness-of-fit test by
comparing the distribution of the unnormalized direction vector to
its true distribution, but also does not test the independence
between the length and the direction vector. In summary,
although the above tests take into account the estimated
mean/covariance, they only test certain aspects of the elliptical
distribution, hence are not omnibus.

Furthermore, there are many tests for elliptical distributions
based on transformations and resampling
or bootstrap approaches, which result in
additional computational cost. For example,

Beran 1979
gives a test statistic based on
transformations of the length and the direction, where, to achieve
the asymptotic results, the number of transformations needs to go to
infinity, while the convergence rate of the test statistic is slower
than
O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
.

Koltchinskii & Sakhanenko 2000
provides a test which requires
taking supremum over a function class that characterizes the
distribution, and the test is conducted using the bootstrap
distribution.

Zhu & Neuhaus 2003
constructs the test statistic via the integral of a squared empirical process of trigonometric
functions. They use a
resampling distribution by keeping the lengths unchanged but
independently sampling from the uniform distribution on the unit
sphere.

Huffer & Park 2007
constructs a test by slicing the data, which
results in
loss of information. Further, except when the null distribution is
normal, they resort to bootstrap in general elliptical distributions.

Ana M. Bianco & Rodrigues 2017
further considers estimating the
mean and covariance robustly for the test statistic in
Zhu & Neuhaus 2003
, but still applies the resampling technique.

Su 2012
also derives a smooth test based on Cholesky
decomposition and spherical harmonics, but also applies the
resampling technique to conduct the test.
Finally,
Albisetti et al. 2020
provides a Kolmogorov-Smirnov type test,
which requires many projecting transformations on the data, and also
utilizes the bootstrap distribution.
Some of the tests for elliptical distribution mentioned above are
summarized and implemented in R
(
Babić, Ley & Palangetić 2021
)
.

There are also some tests that are related to
elliptical distributions. Some of them are designed to
test whether the
data are symmetric about one point.
For example,
Heathcote et al. 1995
introduces a test for
whether a multivariate distribution is symmetric about a point using
a stochastic process via the empirical characteristic function.

Neuhaus & Zhu 1998
;
Henze et al. 2003
propose different
tests for whether the distribution is symmetric about an unspecified
point based on the vanishing imaginary part of the characteristic
function, and conduct the tests via a permutation procedure.
Based on the similar idea,
Ngatchou-Wandji 2009

conducted a Cramér-von Mises test using the asymptotic
distribution.
Also,
Cassart et al. 2008
proposes a pseudo-Gaussian test
which is optimal against some violations of Fechner-type symmetry.
On the other hand, some tests are specific to some specific type of
distribution rather than general elliptical distributions.
For example,
Batsidis & Zografos 2013
and

Ducharme & Lafaye de
Micheaux 2020
propose different classes of
goodness-of-fit tests for several specific types of elliptical
distributions, with some specific density generators. Their null
hypothesis is a specific type of distributions rather than the
general elliptical distribution, and their test statistic
construction also relies on the specific density generator.

In addition, there are a few other works
that cannot be classified into the categories above. Specifically,

Li et al. 1997
provides a QQ-plot based procedure to check spherical and
elliptical distributions visually.

Sakhanenko 2008
conducts comparison studies between
three types tests in
Koltchinskii & Sakhanenko 2000
and three
tests given respectively by

Beran 1979
;
Manzotti et al. 2002
and
Huffer & Park 2007
.

Recently,
Tang & Li 2024
points out that a consistent
test for a spherical distribution should take into account both of
its two characterizing properties:
(i) the length and the direction of the random vector
are independent, and (ii) the direction of the random vector is uniformly
distributed on the unit sphere, and that relying only on one of the
them would lead to false acceptance. Correspondingly, a consistent
test for the elliptical distribution should take into account the
counterparts of these two conditions in the elliptical setting.
Based on the two conditions,
Tang & Li 2024
proposes a
nonparametric test for elliptical distributions by embedding two
distributions – the empirical joint distribution and a factorized
distribution derived from the two conditions – into a tensor product
of reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS), and then computing the
Hilbert-Schmidt
distance between two operators.
The distance is close to zero under elliptical distribution, but is
large if at least one of the two conditions are
violated. However, the procedure critically relies on the
Fréchet differentiability at the null distribution, which may not
be satisfied by all elliptical distributions.

In this paper, we propose a Kullback-Leibler (KL)-divergence (also known as relative
entropy) based testing procedure. KL-divergence is first introduced in

Kullback & Leibler 1951
. KL-divergence is widely
used in many fields to quantify the difference between two
distributions, for example in information theory (see, for
example, Chapters 2 and 8 of
Cover & Thomas 2012
), machine
learning (see, for example, Chapter 2 of
Eguchi & Komori 2022
and
Chapters 9 and 10 of
Bishop 2016
) and statistical
inference (see, for example, Chapter 1 of

Pardo 2020
). In particular, the KL-divergence
is sometimes
used for goodness-of-fit tests.
Li et al. 2005

summarizes two classical approaches of goodness-of-fit tests: minimum
cross entropy (MinxEnt) principle (see

Kullback 1959
;
Kullback & Leibler 1951
) and the
Vasicek-Song test via
M
-spacing method (see

Song 2002
;
Vasicek 1976
). Also, KL-divergence is
closely related to many concepts in information theory, such as
entropy (
Shannon 1948
) and mutual information. See
Chapter 2 of
Cover & Thomas 2012
for more details. In particular,
for two random elements, the mutual information is exactly the
KL-divergence between their joint distribution and the product of
their marginal distributions, which characterizes the dependency
between them. Consequently, mutual information can also be used to test
independence (see, for example,

Berrett & Samworth 2019
;
Ai et al. 2024
;
Pethel & Hahs 2014
).

The problem of testing for ellipticity is a combination of a
goodness-of-fit test and an independence test. Compared to the
goodness-of-fit test, our null distribution, the elliptical
distribution, has the additional complexity of containing a nonparametric component, i.e., the distribution of the
length. Compared to the independence test, we have the additional
complexity of testing the suitability of
the spherical distribution for the direction vector. We handle
both complexities in one step by
directly constructing KL-divergence based test statistic, and representing the
KL-divergence by entropies. To this end,
Berrett et al. 2019
gives a
generalized version
of the Kozachenko-Leonenko entropy estimator (
Kozachenko & Leonenko 1987
) by using

k
-nearest neighbor (
k
NN) method,
and
Berrett et al. 2019
establishes its first-order asymptotic properties.

One difficulty in utilizing the
k
NN method in our problem
is that we are faced with computing distance and determining neighbors
on a sphere while the original method is designed for Euclidean
distance. To bypass this complexity, we convert the problem
back to the Euclidean space by engaging mathematical tools to
establish equivalence presentation.
We further derive the asymptotic property of our resulting test
statistic. Different from
Tang & Li 2024
,
where all steps only involve moment estimators, establishing
theoretical properties of the proposed method is much more
challenging. First of all, it is
challenging to establish the Fréchet differentiability for our

k
NN-based estimator, which prohibits us from using many
high-level functional tools.
Therefore, we derive the influence function of
the test statistic as well as the order of the remainder terms
directly. In particular, when the mean vector and the covariance
matrix are unknown, in order to bound the remainder term, we need to
conduct sample splitting and involve transformation and
truncation tools that are mathematically challenging and
difficult.
Secondly, we handle the degeneration of the influence
function under the null hypothesis by creatively debiasing and inflating
variance.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. We first introduce the
problem setting and our estimating approach in Section

2
. We then consider a basic case where the mean
vector and the covariance matrix are known, and give the test
statistic and asymptotic properties in Section

3
. The general case where the mean
vector and the covariance matrix are unknown is studied in Section

4
. We further
propose the debias method in practice due to the degeneration of the
influence function in Section
5
.
The regularity conditions and proofs are placed in the supplementary materials.
We conduct simulation studies in Section

6
, where we also compare our method to

Tang & Li 2024
and five most popular methods in the literature.
We further apply our method to a real
dataset in Section
7
. Finally, we give some
discussions and conclude
the paper in
Section
8
.

2
General Approach

2.1
Problem Setting

We first state our problem and define some notations. Suppose we are
given a sample of
p
-dimensional independent and identically distributed (iid)
random vectors
{\bf X}_{1},\dots,{\bf X}_{n}
with an absolutely
continuous distribution and a probability density function (pdf)
f_{\bf X}({\bf x})
.
Throughout this paper, we assume that the covariance matrix
{\bm{\Sigma}}=\mbox{cov}({\bf X})
exists.
Our interest is in testing whether or not
{\bf X}
follows an elliptical
distribution. First note that a
p
-dimensional
random vector
{\bf X}
has elliptical distribution if and only if its pdf
can be written as

\displaystyle f_{\bf X}({\bf x})=\{\det({\bf W})\}^{-1/2}g\{({\bf x}-{\bm{\mu}})^{\top}{\bf W}^{-1}({\bf x}-{\bm{\mu}})\}

(1)

where
{\bm{\mu}}\in{\mathrm{R}}^{p}
,
{\bf W}\in{\mathrm{R}}^{p\times p}
, and
g
is a function satisfying
\int g({\bf x}^{\top}{\bf x})d{\bf x}=1
.
We assume
{\bf W}
is a strictly positive definite matrix.
Under the model (
1
), we have
E({\bf X})={\bm{\mu}}
and

\mbox{cov}({\bf X})={\bm{\Sigma}}=c{\bf W}
for some constant
c>0
.
Note that (
1
) is not identifiable in that
it always gives the same model if
{\bf W}
is multiplied by
a constant and
g
is scaled accordingly.
For identifiability purpose, a common choice is to replace

{\bf W}
directly by
{\bm{\Sigma}}
and use the corresponding
g
.
Let
{\bf Y}
be the normalized version of
{\bf X}
, i.e.,

{\bf Y}\equiv{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}({\bf X}-{\bm{\mu}})
,
where

{\bm{\Sigma}}
is strictly positive definite. We assume
{\bm{\mu}}
and

{\bm{\Sigma}}
are finite,
and we can always write

f_{\bf X}({\bf x})=\{\det({\bm{\Sigma}})\}^{-1/2}f_{\bf Y}\{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}({\bf x}-{\bm{\mu}})\}
, where
f_{\bf Y}
is the pdf of
{\bf Y}
.
Let
U\equiv\|{\bf Y}\|
,
{\bf V}\equiv{\bf Y}/U
.
It can be verified that
{\bf X}
has elliptical distribution
if and only if
U\;\,\rule[0.0pt]{0.29999pt}{6.69998pt}\hskip-2.5pt\rule[0.0pt]{6.49994pt}{0.29999pt}\hskip-2.5pt\rule[0.0pt]{0.29999pt}{6.69998pt}\;\,{\bf V}
and
{\bf V}
has a uniform distribution on the
p
-dimensional unit sphere

\mathrm{S}^{p-1}
.
Using this fact, the problem of testing
whether
{\bf X}
has an elliptical distribution is equivalently
written as testing

\displaystyle H_{0}:f_{{\bf V}\mid U}(u,{\bf v})=f_{0}({\bf v})\mbox{\ versus \ }H_{a}:H_{0}\text{ is not true, }

(2)

where
f_{{\bf V}\mid U}(u,{\bf v})
is the conditional pdf of
{\bf V}
given
U
, and
f_{0}({\bf v})
is the pdf of the uniform distribution on the
p
-dimensional unit
sphere. Or equivalently,

\displaystyle H_{0}:f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})=f_{U}(u)f_{0}({\bf v})\quad\mbox{versus}\quad H_{a}:\mbox{$H_{0}$ is not true, }

(3)

where
f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})
is the joint pdf of
(U,{\bf V})
.
Note that the pdf
f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})
is with respect to the product measure
\lambda\times\sigma
,
where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure on
(0,\infty)
and
\sigma
is the surface measure
on the unit sphere
\mathrm{S}^{p-1}
. See Section 2.7 of
Folland 1999
or Section 3.2 of
Stein & Shakarchi 2005
for rigorous derivations. The density
f_{0}
has explicit form

\displaystyle f_{0}({\bf v})=c_{p}I(\|{\bf v}\|=1),\ \text{where}\ c_{p}\equiv\Gamma(p/2)/(2\pi^{p/2}).

2.2
Our Approach

Our general approach to the problem is to use the Kullback-Leibler (KL)
divergence, a well known criterion, to quantify the difference between two
distributions. Given
two probability density functions
f_{1}
and
f_{0}
, the
KL divergence between them is defined as

\displaystyle d(f_{1}\|f_{0})\equiv E[\mbox{log}\{f_{1}({\bf X})/f_{0}({\bf X})\}]=\int\mbox{log}\{f_{1}({\bf x})/f_{0}({\bf x})\}f_{1}({\bf x})d{\bf x}.

Note
that the expectation is computed under
f_{0}
and the KL divergence is not
symmetric. To use the KL divergence to perform the test, we certainly
need to approximate the expectation. It will be shown that this boils
down to
estimating the entropies
H({\bf Y})\equiv-E\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf y})\}
and

H(U)\equiv-E\{\mbox{log}f_{U}(u)\}
.

Entropy estimation has been studied in the statistical
literature. Here, we adopt the Kozachenko-Leonenko estimator

(
Kozachenko & Leonenko 1987
)
, as detailed below.
Let
H({\bf Z})\equiv-E\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf Z}({\bf Z})\}
be the entropy
for an arbitrary
d
-dimensional random vector
{\bf Z}
.
Based on the iid data

{\bf Z}_{1},\dots,{\bf Z}_{n}
,
H({\bf Z})
can be
approximated as

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Z})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k}w_{j}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)\rho_{(j),i}^{d}{\cal V}_{d}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right],

(4)

where
k\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
,
w_{1},\dots w_{k}
are weights
that satisfy
\sum_{j=1}^{k}w_{j}=1
,
{\cal V}_{d}=\pi^{d/2}/\Gamma(1+d/2)
,

\psi(z)\equiv\Gamma^{\prime}(z)/\Gamma(z)
is the digamma function, and

\rho_{(j),i}^{d}\equiv\|{\bf Z}_{(j),i}-{\bf Z}_{i}\|_{2}^{d}
, where
{\bf Z}_{(1),i},\dots,{\bf Z}_{(n-1),i}
is a permutation of
\{{\bf Z}_{1},\dots,{\bf Z}_{n}\}
excluding
{\bf Z}_{i}
such that
\|{\bf Z}_{(1),i}-{\bf Z}_{i}\|\leq\dots\leq\|{\bf Z}_{(n-1),i}-{\bf Z}_{i}\|
. We have the freedom to choose
k,\{w_{j}\}

and the optimal choices are given in
Berrett et al. 2019
. This
method first uses a weighted
k
-nearest neighbors to
approximate
f_{{\bf Z}}({\bf z})
by

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{{\bf Z}}({\bf z}_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}w_{j}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)\rho_{(j),i}^{d}{\cal V}_{d}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right],

and then replaces expectation
E
by sample average. The nearest
neighbor is in terms of Euclidean distance.

Since
[0,\infty)
is a subset of
{\mathrm{R}}
, we can use the
Kozachenko-Leonenko estimator to directly
obtain
\widehat{E}\{\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)\}=-\widehat{H}_{n}(U)
.
However, to construct a similar estimator of
E_{U,{\bf V}}\{\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(U,{\bf V})\}
,
it is difficult to define the nearest neighbor on
(0,\infty)\times\mathrm{S}^{p-1}
.
To solve this problem, we transform the parametrization
(U,{\bf V})
back to the original
{\bf Y}
,
which is in
{\mathrm{R}}^{p}
, and use the Kozachenko-Leonenko estimator to obtain

\widehat{E}\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf Y})\}=-\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Y})
. We first derive the relationship between

f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})
and
f_{\bf Y}(u{\bf v})
in the following lemma.

Lemma
1
.

Let
{\bf Y}
be a random vector in
{\mathrm{R}}^{p}
, and let
U=\|{\bf Y}\|
and
{\bf V}={\bf Y}/U
. Suppose that the pdf of
{\bf Y}
is
f_{\bf Y}({\bf y})
with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathrm{R}}^{p}
, and the joint pdf of
(U,{\bf V})
is
f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})
with respect to
\lambda\times\sigma
, where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure on
(0,\infty)
and
\sigma
is the surface measure on the unit sphere
\mathrm{S}^{p-1}
. Then,
f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})=f_{\bf Y}(u{\bf v})u^{p-1}
.

The proof of Lemma
1
is placed in Section

S.2
of the supplementary materials.
Based on Lemma
1
, we can derive the relationship between
E_{U,{\bf V}}\{\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(U,{\bf V})\}
and
E_{\bf Y}\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf Y})\}
as follows:

\displaystyle E_{U,{\bf V}}\{\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(U,{\bf V})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\bf Y}[\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}\{U({\bf Y}),{\bf V}({\bf Y})\}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\bf Y}\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf Y})\|{\bf Y}\|^{p-1}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\bf Y}\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf Y})\}+(p-1)E_{\bf Y}\{\mbox{log}\|{\bf Y}\|\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\bf Y}\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf Y})\}+(p-1)E_{U}\{\mbox{log}(U)\}.

As is mentioned before, we can estimate the first term using the Kozachenko-Leonenko
estimator. The second term can be estimated by the sample average,
i.e.,

\displaystyle(p-1)\widehat{E}_{n}\{\mbox{log}(U)\}=(p-1)n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mbox{log}(u_{i}).

This allows us to calculate

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{n}(U,{\bf V})=-\widehat{E}_{n}\{\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(U,{\bf V})\}=\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Y})-(p-1)\widehat{E}_{n}\{\mbox{log}(U)\}.

(5)

3
Test with known mean and covariance

To set the stage for the main idea, we start by considering a possibly overly
simplified situation, where

{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
are both known. In this case, we directly observe

{\bf Y}_{i}
and subsequently also directly observe
U_{i},{\bf V}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
.

3.1
Test Statistic Construction

We adopt the hypotheses forms in (
3
) to devise our test.
First, the KL divergence between
f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})
and

f_{U}(u)f_{0}({\bf v})
is calculated as

\displaystyle d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left\{\mbox{log}\frac{f_{U,{\bf V}}(U,{\bf V})}{f_{U}(U)f_{0}({\bf V})}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{S}^{p-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mbox{log}\frac{f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})}{f_{U}(u)f_{0}({\bf v})}f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})dud\sigma({\bf v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\{\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(U,{\bf V})\}-E\{\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)\}-E\{\mbox{log}f_{0}({\bf V})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\{\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(U,{\bf V})\}-E\{\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)\}-\mbox{log}c_{p}.

Remark
1
.

If we replace
f_{0}({\bf v})
with
f_{\bf V}({\bf v})
in the definition of

d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})
, then it is the mutual
information
I(U,{\bf V})
, which is used to measure the dependence between

U
and
{\bf V}
.
So we can write

d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})=I(U,{\bf V})+E\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf V}({\bf V})\}-E\{\mbox{log}f_{0}({\bf V})\}=I(U,{\bf V})+d(f_{\bf V}\|f_{0})
. This indicates that

d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})
consists of two nonnegative components, one measures the
dependence relation, the other measures the deviation of
f_{\bf V}
to

f_{0}
. If
U
and
{\bf V}
are independent, but
{\bf V}
is not necessarily
uniformly distributed on the unit sphere, then
I(U,{\bf V})=0
while

d(f_{\bf V}\|f_{0})>0
. On the other hand, if
{\bf V}
is marginally
uniformly distributed on the unit sphere, but
U
and
{\bf V}
are
dependent, then
d(f_{\bf V}\|f_{0})=0
but
I(U,{\bf V})>0
.
Under
H_{0}
, both are zero and hence
d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})=0
. Otherwise,

d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})>0
.

Remark
2
.

Our test procedure is very different from both
Berrett et al. 2019
and

Berrett & Samworth 2019
, and is far from a simple extension.
In terms of the problem treated,
Berrett et al. 2019

provides an estimator of a single entropy and derives its asymptotic
properties.
On the other hand,
Berrett & Samworth 2019
exclusively considered an
independence testing problem.
We considered a different problem, where we test elliptical
distribution, which involves simultaneously testing
whether the marginal distribution of a properly scaled variable set
is uniformly distributed on the unit sphere and whether two sets of variables are
independent. In addition, our methodology is also different.

Berrett et al. 2019
developed a nonparametric estimation procedure which
does not involve testing methodology.
Berrett & Samworth 2019

relies on resampling to form the testing methodology.
In contrast, we derive the asymptotic properties and rely on the
theoretical properties to develop the testing methodology, as
established in the following development.

Using (
5
),
we define the test statistic to be

\displaystyle T\equiv-\widehat{H}_{n}(U,{\bf V})+\widehat{H}_{n}(U)-\mbox{log}c_{p}=-\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Y})+(p-1)\widehat{E}_{n}\{\mbox{log}(U)\}+\widehat{H}_{n}(U)-\mbox{log}c_{p},

(6)

where
\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Y})
and
\widehat{H}_{n}(U)
are given by
(
4
) with
{\bf Z}
replaced by
{\bf Y}
and
U
.
Specifically,

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{p}}w_{pj}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)\|{\bf Y}_{(j),i}-{\bf Y}_{i}\|^{p}{\cal V}_{p}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right],

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{n}(U)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}}w_{1j}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)|U_{(j),i}-U_{i}|{\cal V}_{1}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right],

where
{\bf Y}_{(j),i}
and
U_{(j),i}
are the
j
-th nearest neighbor of

{\bf Y}_{i}
and
U_{i}
, respectively. Note that the numbers (
k_{p},k_{1}
) of
the nearest neighbors
and the weights (
w_{pj},w_{1j}
) for
\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Y})
and
\widehat{H}_{n}(U)

can be different.

3.2
Asymptotic Properties

Next, we develop the asymptotic distribution of

T
. To this end, we note that
Berrett et al. 2019
gives the asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle n^{1/2}\{\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Z})-H({\bf Z})\}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{-\mbox{log}f_{\bf Z}({\bf z}_{i})-H({\bf Z})\}+o_{p}(1),

(7)

which allows us to get the asymptotic property of
T
under
H_{0}
or

H_{a}
directly. Specifically, under
H_{0}
,

\displaystyle n^{1/2}T

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf y}_{i})+(p-1)\mbox{log}(u_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(u_{i})

\displaystyle-E\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf Y})\}-(p-1)E\{\mbox{log}(U)\}+E\{\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)\}]+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}({\bf u}_{i},{\bf v}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(u_{i})-E\{\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(U,{\bf V})\}+E\{\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)\}]+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\mbox{log}f_{0}({\bf v}_{i})-\mbox{log}c_{p}\}+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{p}(1).

By
Berrett et al. 2019
, there are choices of
k
in (
4
) so that
the above
o_{p}(1)
term is
O_{p}(n^{-c})
for some constant
c>0
.
This is an estimator with convergence rate faster than
n^{-1/2}
,
which is to our benefit. However, in practice, we suggest to approximate
the variance using the formula under alternative derived in (
8
).
Under the alternative,

\displaystyle n^{1/2}\{T-d(f_{U,V}\|f_{U}f_{0})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(u_{i},{\bf v}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(u_{i})-E\{\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(U,{\bf V})\}+E\{\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)\}]+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{(p-1)\mbox{log}u_{i}+\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf y}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(u_{i})+H(U,{\bf V})-H(U)\}+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[(p-1)\mbox{log}(u_{i})+\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf y}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(u_{i})+H({\bf Y})-(p-1)E\{\mbox{log}(U)\}-H(U)]

\displaystyle+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle N\left(0,E[(p-1)\mbox{log}(U)+\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf Y})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)+H({\bf Y})-(p-1)E\{\mbox{log}(U)\}-H(U)]^{2}\right).

The above analysis allows us to perform test using

\displaystyle n\widehat{\mbox{var}}(T)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\sum_{j=1}^{k_{p}}w_{pj}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)\|{\bf Y}_{(j),i}-{\bf Y}_{i}\|^{p}{\cal V}_{p}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right]+\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}}w_{1j}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)|U_{(j),i}-U_{i}|{\cal V}_{1}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right]\right.

(8)

\displaystyle\left.+(p-1)\mbox{log}(u_{i})+\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Y})-(p-1)\widehat{E}(\mbox{log}U)-\widehat{H}_{n}(U)\right)^{2}+n^{-c}.

Under
H_{0}
,
n^{1/2}T/\sqrt{n\widehat{\mbox{var}}(T)}=O_{p}(n^{-c/2})
,
while under the alternative, it has an approximate
normal
distribution with mean
n^{1/2}d(f_{U,V}\|f_{U}f_{0})>0
variance 1. We
summarize the results in Theorem
1
below.
This result allows us to perform a score type test or a
Wald type test, which are capable of detecting a local alternative with
its KL divergence of the order
n^{-1/2}
from the null distribution.

Theorem
1
.

Let
T
be given in (
6
), where
k_{1}
and
w_{1j},j=1,\dots,k_{1}
satisfy the requirements in Theorem 1 of

Berrett et al. 2019
with respect to
f_{U}
, and
k_{p}
and
w_{pj},j=1,\dots,k_{p}
satisfy these requirements with respect to
f_{\bf Y}
.
See also Section
S.1
in the supplementary materials for
these specific requirements.
Then

\displaystyle n^{1/2}\{T-d(f_{U,V}\|f_{U}f_{0})\}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(u_{i},{\bf v}_{i})+o_{p}(1)

where

\displaystyle\psi(u,{\bf v})=\{\mbox{log}f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(u)+H(U,{\bf V})-H(U)\}.

Thus, under
H_{0}
,
n^{1/2}T=o_{p}(1)
and under
H_{a}
,

n^{1/2}\{T-d(f_{U,V}\|f_{U}f_{0})\}\to N[0,E\{\psi(U,{\bf V})^{2}\}]
in distribution.

Remark
3
.

The proof of Theorem
1
is a direct application of Theorem
1 of
Berrett et al. 2019
, hence is omitted.
To ensure the conditions of Theorem 1
of
Berrett et al. 2019
,
we require the orders of
k_{1}
and
k_{p}
to
depend on the smoothness parameters of the function classes containing

f_{U}
and
f_{\bf Y}
. After determining
k_{1}
and

k_{p}
, we can proceed to set the weights
w_{1j}
’s and

w_{pj}
’s to satisfy the
requirements explicitly stated in
Berrett et al. 2019
.
In other words, as long as the function classes of

f_{U},f_{\bf Y}
are sufficiently smooth, we can proceed to choose suitable

k_{1},k_{p}
and the weights so that the conditions are satisfied.

Let the variance of dominating term

\psi(u_{i},{\bf v}_{i})
be
\sigma_{1}^{2}
and the variance contributed from the
residual
o_{p}(1)
term be
\sigma_{2}^{2}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}^{2}=\mbox{var}\left\{n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(u_{i},{\bf v}_{i})\right\},\quad\sigma_{2}^{2}=\mbox{var}\left[n^{1/2}\{T-d(f_{U,V}\|f_{U}f_{0})\}-n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(u_{i},{\bf v}_{i})\right].

It is natural to approximate the
variance of
T
using the estimated version

\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}
. Under
H_{a}
,
\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}
will lead to a
good approximation of
\sigma_{1}^{2}
, which dominates
\mbox{var}(T)
,
while under
H_{0}
,

\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}>\sigma_{1}^{2}

since
\sigma_{1}^{2}=0
, hence hopefully
\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}\geq\mbox{var}(T)
as
well.
However, contrary to our expectation, in our implementation, we find
that this practice often
leads to an underestimated variance, especially under
H_{0}
. Our
suspicion is that the variance
\sigma_{2}^{2}
is not so small under finite
sample size
n
although in theory it is ignorable, and in fact

\sigma_{2}^{2}>\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}
.
To ensure that we maintain the test level, we hence intentionally over
estimate the variance of
T
by overestimating
\sigma_{1}^{2}
.

3.3
Variance inflation

Note that by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\mbox{var}\{\psi({\bf X},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})\}\leq\sigma^{2}\equiv 2\left(V_{1}+V_{2}\right),

where

\displaystyle V_{1}=\mbox{var}\{\mbox{log}f_{{\bf Y}}({\bf Y})\},\quad V_{2}=\mbox{var}\{(p-1)\mbox{log}(U)-\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)\}.

We can estimate
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
respectively by

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k_{p}}w_{pj}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)\|{\bf Y}_{(j),i}-{\bf Y}_{i}\|^{p}{\cal V}_{p}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right]-\widehat{H}_{n}({\bf Y})\right)^{2},

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left((p-1)\left[\mbox{log}(U_{i})-\widehat{E}\{\mbox{log}(U)\}\right]+\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}}w_{1j}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)|U_{(j),i}-U_{i}|{\cal V}_{1}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right]-\widehat{H}_{n}(U)\right)^{2}.\quad

(9)

Thus, we can get a
(1-\alpha)
-level confidence interval for
d(f_{U,V}\|f_{U},f_{0})
as

\displaystyle\left[T-n^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma},+\infty\right),

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\equiv 2(\widehat{V}_{1}+\widehat{V}_{2}).

(10)

Under
H_{0}
, for a specified size
\alpha
, our decision rule will be rejecting
H_{0}
if
T-n^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}>0
.
The p-value can be defined as
1-\Phi(n^{1/2}T/\widehat{\sigma})
, where
\Phi
is the cdf of
N(0,1)
.
The next proposition shows that, after the variance inflation
step, our method can asymptotically control the size while preserve
the power. The proof of the proposition is in the supplementary materials.

Proposition 1
.

Let
T
be given in (
6
), and
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
be given in (
10
) with
\widehat{V}_{1},\widehat{V}_{2}
defined by (
3.3
).
Under the same assumptions as in Theorem
1
, for any

0<\alpha<1/2
, when
n
is sufficiently large,
under
H_{0}
,

\mbox{pr}(\mathrm{reject}\,H_{0})\leq\alpha
,
under
H_{a}
,
\mbox{pr}(\mathrm{reject}\,H_{0})\to 1
.

4
Test with estimated mean and covariance

4.1
Test Statistic Construction

We now consider the realistic situation where
{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}}

are unknown and need to be estimated.
A most natural thing is to estimate
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
so as to
obtain the estimated
{\bf Y}_{i}
’s and then proceed with the testing
procedure described in Section
3
. However, to avoid the
dependence among the normalized observations induced by
\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}
, we split the data into two subsets with sample sizes
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
, respectively. We use the first
n_{1}
observations to estimate
{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}}
, and then form
\widehat{\bf Y}_{i},\widehat{U}_{i},\widehat{\bf V}_{i}
in the last
n_{2}

observations and perform the test. Specifically,
we form
the sample mean and variance
\widehat{\bm{\mu}}=n_{1}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}{\bf X}_{j}
and

\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}=n_{1}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}({\bf X}_{j}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}})^{\otimes 2}
. Subsequently,
we let

\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}=\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}({\bf X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}})
,
\widehat{U}_{i}=\|\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}\|
and

\widehat{\bf V}_{i}=\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}/\widehat{U}_{i}
for
i=n_{1}+1,\dots,n
.
We now define the test statistic to be

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\widehat{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{U},\widehat{\bf V})+\widehat{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{U})-\mbox{log}c_{p}

(11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\widehat{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{\bf Y})+(p-1)\widehat{E}_{n_{2}}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}+\widehat{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{U})-\mbox{log}c_{p}

based on the data
(\widehat{U}_{i},\widehat{\bf V}_{i}),i=n_{1}+1,\dots,n
.

4.2
Asymptotic Properties

We further obtain the asymptotic distribution of
T_{1}
. The
asymptotic expansion of
T_{1}
is established in Theorem

2
.

Theorem
2
.

Let
T_{1}
be as given in (
11
), where
k_{1}
and

w_{1j},j=1,\dots,k_{1}
satisfy the requirements in Theorem 1 of
Berrett et al. 2019

with respect to
f_{U}
, and
k_{p}
and
w_{pj},j=1,\dots,k_{p}

also satisfy the
requirements with respect to
f_{\bf Y}
. See also Section
S.1
in the supplementary materials for
these specific requirements.
Under the regularity conditions A, B, C, D, E, which are stated in Section
S.4
of the supplementary materials, there
exists
k\geq 2
, so that

\displaystyle T_{1}-d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n_{1}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}\psi_{1}({\bf x}_{j},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})+n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i=n_{1}+1}^{n}\psi_{2}({\bf x}_{i},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle+o_{p}(n_{2}^{-1/2}+n_{1}^{-1/2})+O_{p}(n_{2}n_{1}^{-k}),

where

\displaystyle\psi_{1}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}\left(\left[(p-1)E\left({\bf V}{\bf V}^{\top}\right)-{\bf I}-E\left\{\frac{d\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)}{dU}U{\bf V}{\bf V}^{\top}\right\}\right]{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}}){\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle-\left[(p-1)E({\bf V}^{\top}/U)-E\left\{\frac{d\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)}{du}{\bf V}^{\top}\right\}\right]{\bf y},

\displaystyle\psi_{2}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf y})+(p-1)\mbox{log}(u)-\mbox{log}f_{U}(u)+H({\bf Y})-(p-1)E\{\mbox{log}(U)\}-H(U),

\displaystyle{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{dvec}\{-({\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\otimes{\bm{\Sigma}}+{\bm{\Sigma}}\otimes{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2})^{-1}\mbox{vec}({\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}}))\},

\displaystyle{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\bf x}-{\bm{\mu}})^{\otimes 2}-{\bm{\Sigma}}.

Remark
4
.

We can see that the estimation error of
T_{1}
consists of two terms,
the first term captures the error caused by estimating

{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
, hence is of the order
n_{1}^{-1/2}
,
while the second term is the error caused by estimating the
entropies via the Kozachenko-Leonenko estimator, which is performed
using
n_{2}
observations hence is of the order
n_{2}^{-1/2}
. Note that

\psi_{2}({\bf x}_{i},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})
is identical to the influence function in
Theorem
1
.

Highlight of Proof of Theorem
2
:

While we provide the complete proof rigorously in Section

S.6
in the supplementary materials, we provide a summary of the
proof and highlight the main difficulties overcome here.
Our goal is to find the asymptotic expansion of the three terms:

\widehat{E}_{n_{2}}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}-E\{\mbox{log}(U)\}
,
-\widehat{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{\bf Y})+H({\bf Y})
, and
\widehat{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{U})-H(U)
. We discuss the three terms
separately.

On expanding
\widehat{E}_{n_{2}}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}-E\{\mbox{log}(U)\}
, it is
crucial to find the asymptotic expansion of

n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i=n_{1}+1}^{n}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U}_{i})-\mbox{log}(U_{i})\}
. It is then natural to link the term

\mbox{log}(\widehat{U}_{i})-\mbox{log}(U_{i})
to
\widehat{U}_{i}-U_{i}
by Taylor expansion,
and further link to
\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}-{\bf Y}_{i}
. However, two issues appear in
this procedure: (i) we need to guarantee that the average of the
individual ignorable terms in the expansion of
\widehat{U}_{i}-U_{i}
or
\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}-{\bf Y}_{i}
are still ignorable; and (ii) the Lagrange form remainder
involves a term
\frac{1}{\widetilde{U}_{i}^{2}}
as a coefficient, where
\widetilde{U}_{i}
is between
U_{i}
and
\widehat{U}_{i}
, and this term will be
uncontrollable when
\widehat{U}_{i}
is too small.

To deal with issue (i), we retain all residual terms
explicitly in
\widehat{U}_{i}-U_{i}
or
\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}-{\bf Y}_{i}
, and directly show
that each term is ignorable after averaging. To solve
issue (ii), instead of the Lagrange form remainder, we utilize the
integral form remainder in the Taylor expansion. Also, we truncate
each
\widehat{U}_{i}
at
\epsilon_{n}U_{i}
for some small
\epsilon_{n}
. On one
hand, under the nice event where none of
\widehat{U}_{i}
’s are
less than
\epsilon_{n}U_{i}
, we can bound the remainder and
derive its order. On the other hand, we show that the
complementary event, where some
\widehat{U}_{i}
is extremely
small, is sufficiently rare to be ignored. Combining the two parts
gives the final order of the remainder term.

On expanding
-\widehat{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{\bf Y})+H({\bf Y})
, we mainly need to expand
two parts:
n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i=n_{1}+1}^{n}\{\mbox{log}f_{\widehat{\bf Y}}(\widehat{\bf y}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}(\widehat{\bf y}_{i})\}
and
n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i=n_{1}+1}^{n}\{\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}(\widehat{\bf y}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf y}_{i})\}
. On expanding the first part, we first link
\mbox{log}f_{\widehat{\bf Y}}({\bf y})
to
\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf y})
through variable transformation and
Taylor expansion, and further bound the remainder terms in
\mbox{log}f_{\widehat{\bf Y}}(\widehat{\bf y}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}(\widehat{\bf y}_{i})
using the Lipschitz conditions
on the second derivative. The second part can be treated similarly
through Taylor expansion, and the remainder term in
\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}(\widehat{\bf y}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf y}_{i})
can be similarly bounded.

Similar to the previous term, on expanding
\widehat{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{U})-H(U)
, we mainly need to expand two parts:
n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i=n_{1}+1}^{n}\{\mbox{log}f_{\widehat{U}}(\widehat{u}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(\widehat{u}_{i})\}
and
n_{2}^{-1}\sum_{i=n_{1}+1}^{n}\{\mbox{log}f_{U}(\widehat{u}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(u_{i})\}
. In the first term, linking a single

f_{\widehat{U}}(u)
to
f_{U}(u)
requires transforming back to integrals
related to
f_{\bf Y}({\bf y})
, which adds to the complexity of the remainder
terms in a single expansion of
\mbox{log}f_{\widehat{U}}(\widehat{u}_{i})-\mbox{log}f_{U}(\widehat{u}_{i})
. Afterwards, we again encounter the two issues as
in expanding
\widehat{E}_{n_{2}}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}-E\{\mbox{log}(U)\}
, and similar
techniques are applied to solving them. In particular, for the second
issue, here we do a truncation on each
f_{\widehat{U}}(\widehat{u}_{i})
and show
the similar two parts as before. The second part is also handled by
Taylor expansion, where we use the Lipschitz condition and also
conduct further analysis on the ignorable terms.
Combining the three terms leads to the desired result.∎

Theorem
2
also indicates that the convergence rate of
T_{1}-d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})
relies on the sample sizes of both subsets and is
determined by the smaller subsample size. Thus, a
natural choice is to set
n_{1}=n_{2}=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, which leads to

n_{1}^{1/2}\{T_{1}-d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})\}=n_{1}^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}\psi_{1}({\bf x}_{j},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})+n_{2}^{-1/2}\sum_{i=n_{1}+1}^{n}\psi_{2}({\bf x}_{i},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})+o_{p}(1)
.
To compensate for the lost power caused by the reduced sample size due
to the data splitting, we reverse
the roles of the two subsets of the data to obtain
T_{2}
and form

T=(T_{1}+T_{2})/2
. Specifically,
T
can be represented by

\displaystyle T=-\overline{H}_{n}(\widehat{\bf Y})+(p-1)\overline{E}_{n}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}+\overline{H}_{n}(\widehat{U})-\mbox{log}c_{p},

(12)

where

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{n}(\widehat{\bf Y})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\overline{H}_{n_{1}}(\widehat{\bf Y})+\overline{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{\bf Y})\}/2,

\displaystyle\overline{E}_{n}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\overline{E}_{n_{1}}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}+\overline{E}_{n_{2}}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}]/2,

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{n}(\widehat{U})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\overline{H}_{n_{1}}(\widehat{U})+\overline{H}_{n_{2}}(\widehat{U})\}/2.

(13)

The final test statistic has the property established in
Theorem
3
.

Theorem
3
.

Under the same conditions as in Theorem
2
,
the test statistic
T
satisfies

n^{1/2}\{T-d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})\}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi({\bf x}_{i},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})+o_{p}(1),

where

\psi({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})=\psi_{1}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})+\psi_{2}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})
.
Under
H_{0}
,
n^{1/2}T=o_{p}(1)
, and
under
H_{a}
,

\displaystyle n^{1/2}\{T-d(f_{U,{\bf V}}\|f_{U}f_{0})\}\to N[0,E\{\psi({\bf X},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})^{2}\}]

in distribution when
n\to\infty
.

In practice, we can perform test using

\displaystyle n\widehat{\mbox{var}}(T)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\widehat{\psi}_{1}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})+\widehat{\psi}_{2}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})\right)^{2}+n^{-c},

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{1}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}\left(\left[(p-1)n^{-1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}^{\top}-{\bf I}-n^{-1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\frac{\widehat{f}_{U}^{\prime}(\widehat{U}_{j})}{\widehat{f}_{U}(\widehat{U}_{j})}\widehat{U}_{j}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}^{\top}\right]\widehat{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle-\left[(p-1)n^{-1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}^{\top}/\widehat{U}_{j}-n^{-1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\frac{\widehat{f}_{U}^{\prime}(\widehat{U}_{j})}{\widehat{f}_{U}(\widehat{U}_{j})}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}^{\top}\right]\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}({\bf X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{2}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(p-1)\mbox{log}(\widehat{U}_{i})-\sum_{j=1}^{k_{p}}w_{pj}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)\|\widehat{\bf Y}_{(j),i}-\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}\|^{p}{\cal V}_{p}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}}w_{1j}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)|\widehat{U}_{(j),i}-\widehat{U}_{i}|{\cal V}_{1}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right]+\widehat{H}_{n}(\widehat{\bf Y})-(p-1)n^{-1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\mbox{log}\|\widehat{\bf Y}_{j}\|-\widehat{H}_{n}(\widehat{U}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{dvec}\{-(\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\otimes\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}+\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}\otimes\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2})^{-1}\mbox{vec}(\widehat{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}))\},

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\bf X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\mu}})^{\otimes 2}-\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}},

Here, we use the kernel density estimator for
f_{U}
and its derivative as

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{U}(u)=\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{k=1}^{n}K\left(\frac{u-\widehat{U}_{k}}{h}\right),\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{f}_{U}^{\prime}(u)=\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}K^{\prime}\left(\frac{u-\widehat{U}_{k}}{h}\right).

We may set
K
as the Gaussian kernel, i.e.,

K(u)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\exp(-u^{2}/2)
. Note that the corresponding
K^{\prime}(u)=-(2\pi)^{-1/2}\exp(-u^{2}/2)u
.

Remark
5
.

A comparison between Theorems
2
and
3

suggests that the sample size loss
due to sample splitting for
T_{1},T_{2}
is fully recovered in

T
. Similarly, the sample size loss in estimating the entropies is
also fully recovered in
T
. On the other hand, the potential
dependence between the estimation of
{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}}
and the estimation
of the entropies based on the same data is reflected in the fact that

\phi_{1}({\bf x}_{i},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})
and
\phi_{2}({\bf x}_{i},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})
are
evaluated on the same observations in Theorem
3
in
contrast to in Theorem
2
. We thus conjecture that if
we had not performed sample splitting, i.e., if we had performed all the
estimations, including that for
{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}}
and the entropies
using the whole sample, the test statistic would have the same
asymptotic properties. In other words, the sample splitting procedure
does not incur any power loss.

4.3

Variance inflation

Similar to the known
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
case in Section
3.3
, we consider two terms separately:

1.

We know that

\displaystyle n^{1/2}\{-\overline{H}_{n}(\widehat{\bf Y})+H({\bf Y})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{T1}({\bf x}_{i},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})+o_{p}(1)

where

\displaystyle\psi_{T1}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{log}f_{\bf Y}({\bf y})+H({\bf Y})-{\rm tr}\left\{{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}}){\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\right\}.

Let
V_{1}=\mbox{var}\{\psi_{T1}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})\}
. We can estimate
V_{1}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\sum_{j=1}^{k_{p}}w_{pj}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)\|\widehat{\bf Y}_{(j),i}-\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}\|^{p}{\cal V}_{p}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right]+\widehat{H}_{n}(\widehat{\bf Y})\right.

(14)

\displaystyle\left.-{\rm tr}\{{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\}\right)^{2}.

2.

We know that

\displaystyle n^{1/2}(\overline{E}_{n}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}-E\{\mbox{log}(U)\})=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{T2}({\bf x}_{i},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})+o_{p}(1),

and

\displaystyle n^{1/2}\{\overline{H}_{n}(\widehat{U})-H(U)\}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{T3}({\bf x}_{i},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})+o_{p}(1),

where

\displaystyle\psi_{T2}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}\left\{E\left({\bf V}{\bf V}^{\top}\right){\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}}){\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\right\}-E\left({\bf V}^{\top}/U\right){\bf y}+\mbox{log}(u)-E\{\mbox{log}(U)\},

\displaystyle\psi_{T3}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mbox{log}f_{U}(u)-H(U)-{\rm tr}\left[{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}E\left\{\frac{d\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)}{du}U{\bf V}{\bf V}^{\top}\right\}{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})\right]

\displaystyle+E\left\{\frac{d\mbox{log}f_{U}(U)}{du}{\bf V}^{\top}\right\}{\bf y}.

Let
V_{2}=\mbox{var}\{(p-1)\psi_{T2}({\bf X},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})+\psi_{T3}({\bf x},{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\Sigma}})\}
, and we can estimate
V_{2}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left((p-1)\left[{\rm tr}\left\{{\bf A}_{1}\widehat{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\right\}-{\bf A}_{2}\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}+\mbox{log}(\widehat{U}_{i})-\overline{E}_{n}\{\mbox{log}(\widehat{U})\}\right]\right.

(15)

\displaystyle+\left.\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}}w_{1j}\mbox{log}\left[\frac{(n-1)|U_{(j),i}-U_{i}|{\cal V}_{1}}{\exp\{\psi(j)\}}\right]-\widehat{H}_{n}(U)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-{\rm tr}\left\{{\bf B}_{1}\widehat{\bm{\psi}}_{{\bm{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}}({\bf X}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\mu}},\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}})\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\right\}+{\bf B}_{2}\widehat{\bf Y}_{i}\right)^{2}.

where

\displaystyle{\bf A}_{1}=n^{-1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}^{\top},\quad{\bf A}_{2}=n^{-1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}^{\top}/\widehat{U}_{j},

\displaystyle{\bf B}_{1}=n^{-1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\frac{\widehat{f}_{U}^{\prime}(\widehat{U}_{j})}{\widehat{f}_{U}(\widehat{U}_{j})}\widehat{U}_{j}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}^{\top},\quad{\bf B}_{2}=n^{-1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}}\frac{\widehat{f}_{U}^{\prime}(\widehat{U}_{j})}{\widehat{f}_{U}(\widehat{U}_{j})}\widehat{\bf V}_{j}^{\top}.

The confidence interval, decision rule and p-value are the same
as in Section
3.3
, with
\widehat{V}_{1}
and
\widehat{V}_{2}

in
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
replaced by the corresponding quantities above.
Similar to Proposition
1
, the next proposition shows that our test can still control the size while preserve the power after the above variance inflation steps. The proof is in parallel with that of Proposition
1
by directly replacing Theorem
1
by Theorem
3
, and thus is omitted.

Proposition 2
.

Let
T
be given in (
12
), and
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
be given in (
10
) with
\widehat{V}_{1},\widehat{V}_{2}
defined by (
14
) and (
15
), respectively.
For any
0<\alpha<1/2
, when
n
is sufficiently large,
under
H_{0}
,

\mbox{pr}(\mathrm{reject}\,H_{0})\leq\alpha
,
and under
H_{a}
,
\mbox{pr}(\mathrm{reject}\,H_{0})\to 1
.

5

Debiasing

Under
H_{0}
, the first-order influence function of
\widehat{T}
is zero,
while the bias in estimation is usually only guaranteed to be

o(n^{-1/2})
, and hence the bias may dominate the standard deviation of

\widehat{T}
. Even under
H_{0}
, when bias is ignorable, it is always
desirable to correct the potential bias.
We adopt a resampling technique to perform the debiasing.

Specifically, when
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
are known, for
b=1,\ldots,B

and
i=1,\ldots,n
,
we first independently generate
{\bf V}_{bi}^{*}
from the uniform
distribution on the unit sphere, by first generating
a standard normal sample
{\bf Z}_{bi}^{*}
and then setting

{\bf V}_{bi}^{*}={\bf Z}_{bi}^{*}/\|{\bf Z}_{bi}^{*}\|
. We then form

{\bf X}_{bi}^{*}={\bm{\mu}}+{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}U_{i}{\bf V}_{bi}^{*}
.
Thus, for each
b=1,\ldots,B
, we compute a new
test statistic
T_{b}^{*}
using the data
{\bf X}_{b1}^{*},\ldots,{\bf X}_{bn}^{*}
. We then use
\overline{T}_{b}=B^{-1}\sum_{b=1}^{B}T_{b}^{*}
as an estimate
of the bias, and use
T^{\prime}=T-\overline{T}_{b}
as the debiased test statistic.
When
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
are unknown, we follow the same procedure
except that we replace the construction of
{\bf X}_{bi}^{*}
by

{\bf X}_{bi}^{*}=\widehat{\bm{\mu}}+\widehat{\bm{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\widehat{U}_{i}{\bf V}_{bi}^{*}
.

6

Simulations

We consider
p=2,5,10
and
n=500,1000
.
For each
n
and
p
, we select the tuning parameters
k_{p}
and

k_{1}
following Theorem 1 of
Berrett & Samworth 2019
.
Specifically, Theorem 1 of
Berrett et al. 2019
gives the requirements on the order of

k
. We thus determine
k_{1}
and
k_{p}
based on these
order requirements and the finite sample performance.
In terms of the order requirements, we may plug in

\alpha=\beta=\infty
, i.e., we
assume the density is sufficiently smooth and the tail is sufficiently
light. Specifically, we set
k_{p}=\lceil pn^{\tau(p)}\rceil
, where
\tau(p)\leq\min(\frac{2}{5},\frac{4}{4+3p},1-\frac{p/4}{1+\lfloor p/4\rfloor})
. In practice, we find
\tau(1)=\frac{1}{4}
,

\tau(2)=\frac{2}{5}
,
\tau(5)=\frac{4}{19}
, and
\tau(10)=\frac{2}{17}
performs well and we suggest to use these values for
simplicity. When
p\geq 2
, a possibly good choice is

\tau(p)=\min(\frac{4}{4+3p},1-\frac{p/4}{1+\lfloor p/4\rfloor})
, and the above values of
\tau(2)
,
\tau(5)
and
\tau(10)

are calculated based on this. This is to ensure that we have enough
nearest neighbors to use when estimating the entropies, especially
when the dimension
p
is high.
If
p>3
, we select the optimal weights
\{w_{p1},\ldots,w_{pk_{p}}\}

by the function
L2OptW
in the R package
IndepTest

(
Berrett et al. 2018
)
, which, as recommended by
Berrett & Samworth 2019
, minimizes
the Euclidian norm of
(w_{p1},\ldots,w_{pk_{p}})^{\top}
under
the constraints

\mathcal{W}^{(k_{p})}
in (
S.1
).
If
p\leq 3
, we directly set
w_{p1}=\ldots=w_{pk_{p}}=k_{p}^{-1}

to incorporate the information of
more nearest neighbors.
Similarly, we set
w_{11}=\ldots=w_{1k_{1}}=k_{1}^{-1}
.
When
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
are unknown, we set the
bandwidth
h
to be
n^{-1/5}
. We set the resampling sample size as
B=100
.

6.1

Cases with known mean and covariance

We first consider the case when
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
are known.
We consider the following setting to generate data.

Setting 1: For a fixed
skewness parameter

s\in\{0,1,\ldots,p\}
, we generate
{\bf X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p})^{\top}
,
where
X_{1},\ldots,X_{s}
are iid
(\chi^{2}(2)-2)/2
and

X_{s+1},\ldots,X_{p}
are iid
N(0,1)
. Thus, when

s=0
,
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}
are iid
N(0,1)
; when
s=p
,
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}

are iid
(\chi^{2}(2)-2)/2
.

Under Setting 1,
{\bf X}
follows an elliptical distribution
when
s=0
, and deviates from the elliptical distribution as
s

increases. Thus,
s
can be considered as a parameter that
measures the departure from the null distribution, with a larger

s
indicates larger departure from elliptical distribution.

The preliminary simulation results are presented in Figures

1
. As we can see,
when
s=0
, which corresponds to
H_{0}
, the size of our test is well
controlled (all the sizes are below the nominal value 0.05 at the 0.05 test level).
As
s
increases, the empirical power also
increases very sharply. This suggests that the test is consistent
while achieves good power.

Figure 1
:
Simulation results for known
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
in Setting 1.

6.2

Cases with estimated mean and covariance

We now consider the case when
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
are estimated. In
addition to Setting 1 above without using the information of
{\bm{\mu}}

and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
, we further consider the following two settings.

Setting 2: We first generate
U\sim\mathrm{Gamma}(4,2)
,
where the second parameter is the rate, and
{\bf V}\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathrm{S}^{p-1})
. For a fixed parameter
s\in\{0,1,\ldots,p\}
, we generate
W_{j}\sim\mathrm{Gamma}(\gamma_{j},\alpha_{j})
for
j=1,\ldots,s
, with
{\bm{\gamma}}
and
\bm{\alpha}

pre-specified. We then compute
{\bf X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p})^{\top}
, where
X_{j}=UV_{j}/\sqrt{W_{j}}
for
j=1,\ldots,s
, and
X_{j}=UV_{j}
for

j=s+1,\ldots,p
. In practice, we truncate each
W_{j}
on the left at

10^{-3}
to avoid singularity. Regarding the pre-specified
parameters, we set
{\bm{\gamma}}=(0.5,2,0.4,3,0.3,4,0.2,5,0.1,6)^{\top}
and

\bm{\alpha}=(1,2,3,0.1,0.2,0.3,4,5,0.4,0.5)^{\top}
.
Note that when
s=0
,
{\bf X}
has an elliptical distribution.

Setting 3: We first generate
{\bf V}=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{p})^{\top}\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathrm{S}^{p-1})
.
Then based on
{\bf V}
, for a fixed parameter
s\in\{0,1,\ldots,p\}
, we
further generate
U|{\bf V}\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(\sum_{j=1}^{s}j^{2}V_{j}^{2},\sum_{j=1}^{s}j^{2}V_{j}^{2}+1)
. We finally set
{\bf X}=U{\bf V}
.
Note that when
s=0
,
U\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(0,1)
,

{\bf X}
has an elliptical distribution.

Setting 4: We first generate
{\bf Z}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{p})^{\top}\sim t(6)
, the multivariate
t
distribution with mean
{\bf 0}
, shape
matrix
{\bf I}_{p}
and 6 degrees of freedom. We also independently generate

W\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(1/2)
. For a fixed parameter
s\in\{0,1,\ldots,p\}
, we compute
{\bf X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p})^{\top}
where
X_{j}=20W+Z_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,s
, and
X_{j}=Z_{j}
for
j=s+1,\ldots,p
.
Note that when
s=0
,
{\bf X}
has a multivariate t distribution.

Note that, in
Setting 2, both the null and alternatives are centrally
symmetric around
{\bf 0}

(
Babic et al. 2019
)
,
while the alternatives are anisotropic due
to the variety of value combinations in
{\bm{\gamma}}
and
\bm{\alpha}
.
In Setting 3, the independence condition between
U
and
{\bf V}
are
violated under alternative, while
{\bf V}
is still uniformly
distributed on the unit sphere. Setting 4 is the bimodal case, and the
null distribution is similar to Setting 1 but has a heavier tail.
In all four
settings, the parameter
s
measures the deviation from the null
hypothesis.

The simulation results in the four settings above are presented in
Figure
2
. In all cases, when
s=0
, the size of
our test is also well controlled with the maximum size being 0.044
at the 0.05 level test. As we can see from Figure

2
, the empirical power increases with the
departure from the null hypothesis in most cases.
In certain situation, we observe the empirical powers decrease
slightly when
s=p
, possibly because the distribution is more isotropic compared
to the ones when
1\leq s\leq p-1
and hence
hard to distinguish.

Figure 2
:
Simulation results for unknown
{\bm{\mu}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}
in four settings.

6.3

Comparison with existing methods

In this section we compare our method (KL) to the
kernel-embedding-based
test (KE) in
Tang & Li 2024
, as well as five other
tests summarized in
Babić, Ley & Palangetić 2021
, including the tests
proposed by
Huffer & Park 2007
(HP), by
Manzotti et al. 2002

(MPQ) , by
Cassart et al. 2008
(PG), by
Schott 2002
(SW),
and by
Babić, Gelbgras, Hallin & Ley 2021
(SO). The seven abbreviations of the tests will be used below.

We conduct our experiment under the same settings as in
Section
6.2
and repeated the experiments 1000 times in
general. However, one exception is made for the competing method KE,
where we only repeated the experiments 100 times for sample size
1000, due to its extremely large computational cost.

The empirical sizes of the seven tests in the four settings
are summarized in Table
1
, while the rejection rate
as a function of
s
, i.e., the departure level from null, are presented in
Figures
3
,
4
,

5
and
6
.
The exact rejection values are given
in Tables S.1 – S.4 in the supplementary materials.

Figure 3
:
Comparisons of seven methods under Setting 1.

:

:

The results in
Table
1
indicate that the sizes of all methods except HP and KE are
controlled well in all cases. HP has difficulties in
maintaining the size under 0.05 in Settings 2, 3 and 4, likely because the
asymptotic null distribution of HP is based on the multivariate
normal distribution, but our null distributions in these three settings are
elliptical distributions very
different from normal.
The size of KE is not well controlled in Setting 3, and occasionally in Setting 2,
probably because the distribution of
{\bf Y}
is very concentrated
around zero under the two circumstances (especially in Setting 3),
which leads to inaccurate estimation of the direction vector

{\bf V}
. This is the case where the Fréchet differentiability condition in

Tang & Li 2024
is likely violated, leading KE to
mistakenly reject the null hypothesis. In theory, our method
KL should also encounter the similar issue in Setting 3, where
the requirement
E(1/\|{\bf Y}\|^{3})<\infty
is violated
when
U=\|{\bf Y}\|\sim\mathrm{Uniform}(0,1)
. However, KL exhibits
certain robustness in that it still controls the size well after the debiasing
and variance inflation step.

Because a test has to maintain its size in order to
control the type I error and deliver trustworthy decision, hence
we will direct our attention more towards the power comparison
of the remaining five testing procedures: KL, MPQ, PG, SW and SO.
To this end, it
is difficult to
claim an obvious winner in all settings. Figure

3
indicates that in Setting 1, MPQ, PG, SW and SO are
more powerful in general than KL.
This is not a surprise since SO is shown
to be the most powerful test in detecting skew-elliptical distributions

(
Babić, Gelbgras, Hallin & Ley 2021
)
, while it is the skewness that causes the
departure from null in this setting.
Also, as is shown in
Cassart et al. 2008
, PG is optimal
against a type of multivariate Fechner asymmetry, which includes
our alternatives in Setting 1. MPQ is based on the uniform
distribution of
{\bf V}
on the unit sphere. When some
components of
{\bf X}
are changed to
\chi^{2}

distributions, the resulting distribution of
{\bf V}
is very far
from uniform.
SW is based on the structure of the fourth moments of the
distributions. Under the skewness introduced by the
\chi^{2}

distributions, the moment structure deviates very much from
the null. Note that as the departure from null
increases, all five methods are very powerful.

However, Figures

4
,
5
and

6
tell very different stories,
in that SO is the least powerful in Settings 2, 3 and 4. This is because
in these settings, the departure from null is no longer due to the
skewness.
Also, PG does not have good powers in Settings 3 and 4,
possibly due to the similar reason that the Fechner symmetry is not
strongly violated. MPQ does not perform well in Setting 3, because it
only tests the uniform distribution of
{\bf V}
but does not consider the
dependency between
U
and
{\bf V}
. On the other hand, SW does not
perform well in Setting 4, possibly because under the bimodal
setting, the fourth moment structure is not affected much.

Lastly, we comment briefly on the power performance of HP and
KE, the two tests that
do not control the size well in all settings. The powers of HP in Settings 2, 3
and 4 are not good, possibly because the slicing step in HP leads to
much information loss. The powers of KE are generally not good when
the sample size
n=500
, but have some improvement
when the sample size increases from

n=1000
. In addition, KE does not perform well when
the dimension is high. This is probably because the deviations from the
null are in the direction orthogonal to the chosen basis functions.
In contrast, KL controls size well, and retains good power in all settings,
even though it may not be the most powerful in each scenario.

7

Application

In this section, we apply our test to a real dataset on Travel Reviews

(
Renjith 2018
)
. The dataset can be found from

u
rl https://archive.ics.uci.edu/dataset/484/travel+reviews. In this
dataset, there are
n=980
records, each of them including
p=9

features. Note that we exclude category 7 because it is discrete.
Thus,
X_{1},\ldots,X_{9}
correspond to categories 1,2,3,4,5,6,8,9,10.
The scatterplot of the original dataset is given in Figure

S.1
of the supplementary materials.
Applying our test to the original dataset, the p-value is
approximately
2.0994\times 10^{-6}
, which indicates that the
original dataset is very unlikely to be elliptically distributed.

We further perform a Box-Cox transformation on the dataset, using the method
given in Section 7.2 of
Li 2018
. The optimal Box-Cox
transformation parameter

\lambda
’s are:

\displaystyle 0.1053,0.3158,0.1053,-0.5263,0.3158,0.7368,0.5263,0.1053,0.3158.

The scatterplot of the Box-Cox transformed dataset is given in Figure

S.2
of the supplementary materials.

We subsequently apply our test to the Box-Cox transformed dataset, and
obtain the
p-value
6.0511\times 10^{-6}
. This suggests that
the Box-Cox transformed dataset is also unlikely to be elliptically
distributed. This is reasonable because by Section 7.3 of

Li 2018
, the Box-Cox transformation can only improve
elliptical shapes to pairwise distributions of a data set, but highly
non-elliptical shapes may still appear in the joint distribution.

To further investigate the effect of the Box-Cox transformation,
we conduct analysis on the ellipticity of pairwise distributions

(X_{i},X_{j})
for
1\leq i<j\leq p
, both on the original data and the
transformed data. The p-values for the pairwise
comparisons on the original data are shown in the upper triangular part
of Table

2
, and those on the Box-Cox transformed
data are shown in the lower triangular part
of Table

2
.

Table 2
:
P-values for pairwise comparisons on the original data (upper
triangle) and the transformed data (lower triangle).

At the overall level
\alpha=0.05
, we test

H_{0}
:
(X_{i},X_{j})
is elliptically distributed for all the
(i,j)

pairs.
Using Bonferroni correction, we set the individual significant level

\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha/36=0.0014
since there are in total
p(p-1)/2=36
comparisons.
For the original data, the pairs
(X_{i},X_{j})
that violates the
elliptical distribution requirement are

\displaystyle(X_{1},X_{3}),(X_{3},X_{4}),(X_{3},X_{5}),(X_{3},X_{6}),(X_{3},X_{7}),(X_{3},X_{9}).

In contrast, all the pairs pass the test after the Box-Cox
transformation. This result verifies empirically that the Box-Cox
transformation indeed improves the elliptical shape
on the pairwise distributions of the data.

8

Conclusion and Discussion

We have proposed a KL-divergence based test for elliptical
distribution. In assessing the KL-divergence, we convert it to
entropies in the Euclidean space, and estimate the entropies via
k

nearest neighbor methods. We derive the asymptotic properties of the
test statistic when both the mean and covariance of the data are known
and unknown. Due to the
degeneration of the influence function, we have further conducted
debiasing and variance inflating,
which leads to a more conservative test procedure in theory.
Achieving a test at the target level using this method will require
higher order influence function and is worth future studying. However,
based on the simulation
studies, our test achieves better size and power performance than the
state of the art procedures.

In this paper, we considered the fixed
dimension
p
. Things can be very different when
p
is allowed
to increase with
n
. Even when
p
increases very slowly,
all the results need to be reinvestigated, including the theoretical and
numerical properties of the
k
NN-based estimator for the entropy.
When
p
increases sufficiently fast, even the basic procedures such as
estimating the covariance matrix will encounter issues and additional
sparsity assumption will be needed and very different analysis will be
required. Indeed, testing for elliptical distribution in high
dimensional data is challenging, and we are aware of the work

Banerjee & Ghosh 2024
, which discussed this issue and allowed
a rate of departure from the null slower than parametric rate under an
increasing
p
without giving concrete results.
Recently,
Wang & Lopes 2025
proposed an asymptotic goodness-of-fit test
in high dimensions, where
p
and
n
are allowed to diverge proportionally.
Much more in depth analysis is need to investigate the
high dimension testing for elliptical distributions.

Supplementary Materials

The supplementary materials contain regularity
conditions, proofs of the main results, and additional tables and
plots for
simulation results and the real data analysis. Because the proof Theorem
1
is much
simpler than that of Theorems
2
and

3
, we omit its proof.

In the following sections, we
first list the requirements of Theorem 1 of

Berrett et al. 2019

in Section
S.1
.
We then provide the proofs of Lemma
1
and Proposition
1
in Sections
S.2
and
S.3
.
We also
present the proofs of the Theorems
2
and
3
as well as the conditions involved.
The proof of Theorem
2
is lengthy. We first list the
regularity assumptions in Section
S.4
.
We classify these assumptions in five groups
named A, B, C, D, and E, based on where these Assumptions are
used. We then prove some basic lemmas in Section
S.5
,
and prove Theorems
2
and
3
in Sections
S.6
and
S.7
, respectively. Sections
S.8
and
S.9
include some additional tables and
plots for
numerical results.

S.1

List of Requirements in Theorem 1 of

Berrett et al. 2019

Before stating the requirements for
k_{1}
,
k_{p}
,
{\bf w}_{1}
and
{\bf w}_{p}
, we start with some concepts given in
Berrett et al. 2019
. We first give a general statement for dimension
d
. Suppose that
{\bf Z}
is a random vector in
{\mathrm{R}}^{d}
with density function
f
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{d}
be the class of density functions in
{\mathrm{R}}^{d}
with respect to the Lebesgue measure,
and let
\mathcal{A}
be the class of decreasing functions
a:(0,\infty)\to[1,\infty)
such that
a(\delta)=o(\delta^{-\epsilon})
, as
\delta\searrow 0
for all
\epsilon>0
.
Let
\Theta=(0,\infty)^{4}\times\mathcal{A}
, and define the parameter
{\bm{\theta}}=(\alpha,\beta,\nu,\gamma,a)\in\Theta
.

Denote the target function class

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{d,{\bm{\theta}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{f\in\mathcal{F}_{d}:\mu_{\alpha}(f)\leq\nu,\|f\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma,\sup_{{\bf x}:f({\bf x})\geq\delta}M_{f,\alpha,\beta}({\bf x})\leq a(\delta)\mbox{ for }\forall\delta>0\right\},

where

\displaystyle\mu_{\alpha}(f)=\int_{{\mathrm{R}}^{d}}\|{\bf x}\|^{\alpha}f({\bf x})d{\bf x},

for
f\in\mathcal{F}_{d}
and
\alpha>0
, and

\displaystyle M_{f,\alpha,\beta}({\bf x})=\max\left\{\max_{t=1,\dots,m}\frac{\|f^{(t)}({\bf x})\|}{f({\bf x})},\,\sup_{{\bf y}\in B_{\bf x}^{\circ}\{r_{a}({\bf x})\}}\frac{\|f^{(m)}({\bf y})-f^{(m)}({\bf x})\|}{f({\bf x})\|{\bf y}-{\bf x}\|^{\beta-m}}\right\},

where
m=\lceil\beta\rceil-1
, and
r_{a}({\bf x})=[8d^{1/2}a\{f({\bf x})\}]^{-1/(\beta\wedge 1)}
. Note that when we mention
M_{f,\alpha,\beta}({\bf x})
, we assume that
f
is
m
-times differentiable at
{\bf x}
.

We then introduce the weight constraints. For a fixed
k\in\natural
, the class of weights
{\bf w}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{k})^{\top}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{{\bf w}\in{\mathrm{R}}^{k}:\sum_{j=1}^{k}w_{j}\frac{\Gamma(j+2l/d)}{\Gamma(j)}=0\,\mbox{ for }\,l=1,\dots,\lfloor d/4\rfloor,\right.

(S.1)

\displaystyle\left.\sum_{j=1}^{k}w_{j}=1,\,\mbox{ and }\,w_{j}=0\,\mbox{ if }\,j\notin\{\lfloor k/d\rfloor,\lfloor 2k/d\rfloor,\dots,k\}\right\}.

Based on the above concepts, we now state the requirements for
k
and
{\bf w}
as in Theorem 1 of
Berrett et al. 2019
. We set
\alpha>d
and
\beta>d/2
.
We require
k\in\{k_{0}^{*},\dots,k_{1}^{*}\}
,
where
k_{0}^{*}=k_{0,n}^{*}
and
k_{1}^{*}=k_{1,n}^{*}
are defined as two deterministic sequences of positive integers satisfying
k_{0}^{*}\leq k_{1}^{*}
,
k_{0}^{*}/\mbox{log}^{5}n\to\infty
,
k_{1}^{*}=O(n^{\tau_{1}})
and
k_{1}^{*}=o(n^{\tau_{2}})
, where

\displaystyle\tau_{1}<\min\left(\frac{2\alpha}{5\alpha+3d},\frac{\alpha-d}{2\alpha},\frac{4\beta^{*}}{4\beta^{*}+3d}\right),\quad\tau_{2}=\min\left(1-\frac{d/4}{1+\lfloor d/4\rfloor},1-\frac{d}{2\beta}\right),

where
\beta^{*}=\beta\wedge 1
.
In addition, we require
k\geq k_{\min}
, where
k_{\min}\in\natural
satisfies that for each
k^{\prime}\geq k_{\min}
, there exists
{\bf w}={\bf w}^{(k^{\prime})}\in\mathcal{W}^{(k^{\prime})}
with
\sup_{k^{\prime}\geq k_{\min}}\|{\bf w}^{(k^{\prime})}\|<\infty
.
Note that
k_{\min}
depends only on
d
, and its existence is guaranteed by Theorem 1 of
Berrett et al. 2019
.
When the sample size
n
is sufficiently large,
k_{0,n}^{*}\geq k_{\min}
is satisfied.
Also, the asymptotic result in (
7
) holds given that
k\in\{k_{0}^{*},\dots,k_{1}^{*}\}
and
{\bf w}={\bf w}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{W}^{(k)}
(see Theorems 1 and 8 of
Berrett et al. 2019
).

The requirements for of
k_{p}
,
k_{1}
,
{\bf w}_{p}
and
{\bf w}_{1}
are based on the above statements, where
d
is replaced by
p
and
1
, respectively. To be specific, suppose that
f_{U}\in\mathcal{F}_{1,{\bm{\theta}}_{1}}
, where
{\bm{\theta}}_{1}=(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1},\nu_{1},a_{1})
, then we calculate
\tau_{11}
and
\tau_{12}
based on the form of
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
with
\alpha_{1}
,
\beta_{1}
and
d=1
, and the order of the lower and upper bounds for
k_{1}
, namely
k_{10}^{*}
and
k_{11}^{*}
, can be calculated accordingly. Similarly, when
f_{\bf Y}\in\mathcal{F}_{p,{\bm{\theta}}_{p}}
where
{\bm{\theta}}_{p}=(\alpha_{p},\beta_{p},\gamma_{p},\nu_{p},a_{p})
, then we calculate
\tau_{p1}
and
\tau_{p2}
using
\alpha_{p}
,
\beta_{p}
and
d=p
, and the order of the lower and upper bounds for
k_{p}
, namely
k_{p0}^{*}
and
k_{p1}^{*}
, can be calculated similarly. Based on the
k_{1}
and
k_{p}
, we can select
{\bf w}_{1}\in\mathcal{W}^{(k_{1})}
and
{\bf w}_{p}\in\mathcal{W}^{(k_{p})}
.

S.2

Proof of Lemma
1

Define
T:(0,\infty)\times\mathrm{S}^{p-1}\to{\mathrm{R}}^{p}\backslash\{{\bf 0}\},(u,{\bf v})\mapsto u{\bf v}
. Clearly,
T
is a continuous bijection, and
T^{-1}
is also continuous.
For any measurable set
A\in(0,\infty)\times\mathrm{S}^{p-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mbox{pr}\{(U,{\bf V})\in A\}=\int_{\mathrm{S}^{p-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}I_{A}(u,{\bf v})f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})dud\sigma({\bf v}).

Also, using
{\bf Y}=U{\bf V}=T(U,{\bf V})
, we have another representation of
\mbox{pr}\{(U,{\bf V})\in A\}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mbox{pr}\{(U,{\bf V})\in A\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{pr}\{{\bf Y}\in T(A)\}=\int_{{\mathrm{R}}^{p}}I_{T(A)}({\bf y})f_{\bf Y}({\bf y})d{\bf y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{S}^{p-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}I_{T(A)}(u{\bf v})f_{\bf Y}(u{\bf v})u^{p-1}dud\sigma({\bf v})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{S}^{p-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}I_{A}(u,{\bf v})f_{\bf Y}(u{\bf v})u^{p-1}dud\sigma({\bf v}),

where we use Theorem 3.4 of
Stein & Shakarchi 2005
in the second last equality.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{S}^{p-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}I_{A}(u,{\bf v})f_{U,{\bf V}}(u,{\bf v})dud\sigma({\bf v})=\int_{\mathrm{S}^{p-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}I_{A}(u,{\bf v})f_{\bf Y}(u{\bf v})u^{p-1}dud\sigma({\bf v}).

∎

S.3

Proof of Proposition
1

Note that our test rejects
H_{0}
if and only if

T-n^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}>0
.
By Theorem
1
,
under
H_{0}
, we have
n^{1/2}T\to 0
and
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\to\sigma^{2}

in probability. Slutsky’s theorem implies that
n^{1/2}T/\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\to 0
in probability, which implies that

\displaystyle\mbox{pr}(T-n^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}>0)=\mbox{pr}(n^{1/2}T/\widehat{\sigma}>z_{\alpha})\to 0

for any
z_{\alpha}>0
.
Hence
\mbox{pr}(\mathrm{reject}\,H_{0})=\mbox{pr}(T-n^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}>0)<\alpha
for sufficiently large
n
.
Under
H_{a}
, however, we have
T\to d(f_{U,V}\|f_{U},f_{0})>0
and
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\to\sigma^{2}
in probability. Slutsky’s theorem implies that

T/\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\to d(f_{U,V}\|f_{U},f_{0})/\sigma^{2}>0
in
probability, so
n^{1/2}T/\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\to\infty
in
probability. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mbox{pr}(\mathrm{reject}\,H_{0})=\mbox{pr}(T-n^{-1/2}z_{\alpha}\widehat{\sigma}>0)=\mbox{pr}(n^{1/2}T/\widehat{\sigma}>z_{\alpha})\to 1

for any
z_{\alpha}>0
.
∎

S.4

Assumptions
